[00:42:29] I'm Margaret Maliszewski. I'm also with the Planning Division of Community Development, and I would just like to note that in addition to the assistance of the AC44 team on this chapter, Chris Taggart, Mariah Gleason, Scott Clark, and James Van Branken have also contributed to the chapter.

[00:42:47] In this chapter, we tried to make the point that cultural resources really make Albemarle the unique place that it is. Cultural resources establish community character, they contribute to our quality of life, and they support our climate action goals.

[00:43:05] But with all those positive attributes comes some significant challenges. For example, thousands of historic resources have been surveyed, and when I say survey, I mean that technical documentation of the characteristics of the resource, not like a door-to-door questionnaire.

[00:43:23] Thousands of historic resources have been surveyed, but many of those surveys are out of date. Many of the early surveys of historic resources focused on high-style architecture, and they failed to look at the more modest buildings in the county.

[00:43:41] They overlooked sometimes the landscape that the buildings were set in and the social context in which those buildings were developed. Another challenge is the varying degree of protection that's in place for the various resources. Many resources have some level of designation.

[00:44:00] Most of those designations have no protection with them. And that is an example of that is our historic resources and some of the scenic roads. In other cases, protections that are in place might be refined to achieve better results.

[00:44:20] Our entrance quarters are one example of that. And other resources are just challenging in themselves. For example, scenic view sheds, simply due to the size of those resources. So the goal, objectives, and actions in this chapter outline some ways to tackle those issues.

[00:44:41] As a reminder, the Cultural Resources Goal says that Albemarle County will recognize, celebrate, and promote awareness of our resources, and we will actively work to identify, preserve, and protect them. The chapter has six objectives, and they focus on maintaining those accurate surveys, educating about the resources, increasing protection through regulations, and increasing incentives and funding streams.

[00:45:12] One of the themes that we've emphasized in this chapter is this abundance of resources and noting that many of these resources overlap. For example, there might be a historic house that's sitting in a landscape of rolling farmland that might be part of an historic district.

[00:45:32] It might be situated on an entrance corridor and along a scenic stream. And maybe there's some land and conservation easement nearby. While protecting every resource isn't practical, we could look at this abundance of resources more holistically, study how they're layered, and see where the overlapping resources can help set priorities for which resources should be protected and what sorts of regulations might be most efficient and effective.

[06:04:17] And this is during normal or potentially flood conditions. So some of the characteristics of that to consider for this is the hydraulic isolation characteristic, which is backwater areas such as Knoxville Lakes and wetlands that offer a disconnected, to have a limited interaction with the main river flow, creating essentially a backward flow direction in Meadow Creek.

[06:04:52] Adding fuel to this area doesn't significantly alter the main channel's capacity to convey the floodwaters, so the floodplain water elevations remain unaffected. Next characteristic is a low flow velocity.

[06:05:08] These areas experience stagnant or slow-moving water, mainly that they don't contribute substantially to the floodwater movement. This lower velocity is often what you see when you have such an obstruction.

[06:05:25] You'll have a very steel-flowing situation, and that reduces the risk of erosion and other damaging effects that sometimes a massive flood would have. Floodplain storage capacity is primarily determined by the main channel, Again, the Ribana in this situation and adjacent low-lying areas actively involved in flood water conveyance.

[06:05:57] Backwater zones such as this often lie outside of the active zone and some modify them with fill and doesn't reduce the floodplain's ability to store or convey the water. So that just gives you a broad overview of that potential situation.

[07:14:22] regulations, including the county's water protection ordinance, the Clean Water Act if wetlands are affected, and national flood insurance program requirements.
